<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Perugia_Altarpiece,_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg/220px-The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="312" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg/330px-The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg/440px-The_Perugia_Altarpiece%2C_Side_Panel_Depicting_St._Dominic.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1700" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Saint Dominic</a> (1170–1221), portrayed in the <i><a href="/wiki/Perugia_Altarpiece" title="Perugia Altarpiece">Perugia Altarpiece</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Fra_Angelico" title="Fra Angelico">Fra Angelico</a>. <a href="/wiki/Galleria_Nazionale_dell%27Umbria" title="Galleria Nazionale dell&#39;Umbria">Galleria Nazionale dell'Umbria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Perugia" title="Perugia">Perugia</a>.</figcaption></figure> 
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg/220px-Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg/330px-Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg/440px-Figur_Alte_Uni_Marburg.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1944" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption>A figure depicting the term <i>domini canes</i> (lat. "<a href="/wiki/Hound" title="Hound">hounds</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">lord</a>") since the <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Inquisition" title="Medieval Inquisition">Inquisition</a> in the 13th century,<sup id="cite_ref-DC_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DC-2">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Helden1995_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Helden1995-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> on a corner of a former Dominican monastery (before the Reformation), Old University, <a href="/wiki/Marburg" title="Marburg">Marburg</a>, Germany</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <b>Order of Preachers</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ordo Praedicatorum</i>) abbreviated <b>OP</b>, also known as the <b>Dominicans</b>, is a <a href="/wiki/Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic">Catholic</a> <a href="/wiki/Mendicant_order" class="mw-redirect" title="Mendicant order">mendicant order</a> of <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_right" title="Pontifical right">pontifical right</a> founded in France, by a Spanish <a href="/wiki/Priest" title="Priest">priest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Dominic</a>. It was approved by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Honorius_III" title="Pope Honorius III">Pope Honorius III</a> via the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Religiosam_vitam" title="Religiosam vitam">Religiosam vitam</a></i></span> on 22 December 1216. Members of the order, who are referred to as <i>Dominicans</i>, generally carry the letters <i>OP</i> after their names, standing for <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Ordinis Praedicatorum</i></span></i>, meaning <i>of the Order of Preachers</i>. Membership in the order includes <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">friars</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-friar_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-friar-4">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nuns" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuns">nuns</a>, <a href="/wiki/Religious_sister_(Catholic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious sister (Catholic)">active sisters</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Laity" title="Laity">lay</a> or secular Dominicans (formerly known as <a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_Saint_Dominic" title="Third Order of Saint Dominic">tertiaries</a>). More recently there has been a growing number of associates of the religious sisters who are unrelated to the tertiaries.
</p><p>Founded to preach the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">gospel</a> and to oppose <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresy</a>, the teaching activity of the order and its scholastic organisation placed it in the forefront of the intellectual life of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The order is famed for its intellectual tradition by having produced many leading <a href="/wiki/Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Theologian">theologians</a> and philosophers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarshall2011_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall2011-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In 2018, there were 5,747 Dominican friars, including 4,299 priests.<sup id="cite_ref-ch_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ch-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The order is headed by the <a href="/wiki/Master_of_the_Order_of_Preachers" title="Master of the Order of Preachers">master of the order</a> who, as of 2022<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, is <a href="/wiki/Gerard_Timoner_III" title="Gerard Timoner III">Gerard Timoner</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELomonaco2019_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELomonaco2019-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> and <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a> are the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">co-patronesses</a> of the Order.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Foundation">Foundation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Foundation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Doctrina-cristiana.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Doctrina-cristiana.jpg/220px-Doctrina-cristiana.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="296" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Doctrina-cristiana.jpg/330px-Doctrina-cristiana.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Doctrina-cristiana.jpg/440px-Doctrina-cristiana.jpg 2x" data-file-width="476" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Saint Dominic on the front cover of <i><a href="/wiki/Doctrina_Christiana" title="Doctrina Christiana">Doctrina Christiana</a></i> catechism in <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tagalog_language" title="Tagalog language">Tagalog</a> with an eight-pointed star (a symbol of the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a>) over his head. Woodcut cover. Printed in Manila in 1593</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Dominican Order came into being in the Middle Ages at a time when men of God were no longer expected to stay behind the walls of a <a href="/wiki/Cloister" title="Cloister">cloister</a>. Instead, they travelled among the people, taking as their examples the apostles of the primitive Church. Out of this ideal emerged two orders of mendicant friars: one, the Friars Minor, was led by <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>; the other, the Friars Preachers, by <a href="/wiki/Dominic_of_Guzman" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominic of Guzman">Dominic of Guzman</a>. Like his contemporary, Francis, Dominic saw the need for a new type of organization, and the quick growth of the Dominicans and <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscans</a> during their first century of existence confirms that conditions were favorable for the growth of the <a href="/wiki/Mendicant_orders" title="Mendicant orders">orders of mendicant friars</a>. The Dominicans and other mendicant orders may have been an adaptation to the rise of the profit economy in medieval Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELittle1983_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELittle1983-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dominic sought to establish a new kind of order, one that would bring the dedication and systematic education of the older monastic orders like the <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictines</a> to bear on the religious problems of the burgeoning population of cities, but with more organizational flexibility than either monastic orders or the secular clergy. The Order of Preachers was founded in response to a then perceived need for informed preaching.<sup id="cite_ref-dominicanfriars.org_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominicanfriars.org-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Dominic's new order was to be trained to preach in the <a href="/wiki/Vernacular" title="Vernacular">vernacular</a> languages.
</p><p>Dominic inspired his followers with loyalty to learning and virtue, a deep recognition of the spiritual power of worldly deprivation and the religious state, and a highly developed governmental structure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19757_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19757-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> At the same time, Dominic inspired the members of his order to develop a "mixed" spirituality. They were both active in preaching, and contemplative in study, prayer and meditation. The brethren of the Dominican Order were urban and learned, as well as contemplative and mystical in their spirituality. While these traits affected the women of the order, the nuns especially absorbed the latter characteristics and made those characteristics their own. In England, the Dominican nuns blended these elements with the defining characteristics of English Dominican spirituality and created a spirituality and collective personality that set them apart.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dominic_of_Caleruega">Dominic of Caleruega</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Dominic of Caleruega">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Saint Dominic</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg/220px-Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg/330px-Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg/440px-Santo_Domingo_en_oraci%C3%B3n.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1525" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Saint Dominic</a> (1170–1221), portrait by <a href="/wiki/El_Greco" title="El Greco">El Greco</a>, about 1600</figcaption></figure>
<p>As an adolescent, he had a particular love of theology and the Scriptures became the foundation of his spirituality.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197517_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197517-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> During his studies in <a href="/wiki/Palencia" title="Palencia">Palencia</a>, Spain, he experienced a dreadful famine, prompting Dominic to sell all of his beloved books and other equipment to help his neighbours.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell198253_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell198253-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He was made a canon and ordained to the priesthood in the monastery of Santa María de La Vid.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> After completing his studies, Bishop Martin Bazan and <a href="/wiki/Prior_(ecclesiastical)" title="Prior (ecclesiastical)">Prior</a> <a href="/wiki/Diego_de_Acebo" title="Diego de Acebo">Diego de Acebo</a> appointed him to the cathedral chapter of <a href="/wiki/Osma" class="mw-redirect" title="Osma">Osma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197519_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197519-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Preaching_to_the_Cathars">Preaching to the Cathars</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Preaching to the Cathars">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1203, Dominic de Guzmán joined <a href="/wiki/Diego_de_Acebo" title="Diego de Acebo">Diego de Acebo</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Osma" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Osma">Bishop of Osma</a>, on a <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_mission" title="Diplomatic mission">diplomatic mission</a> to Denmark for the monarchy of Spain, to arrange the marriage between the son of King <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_VIII_of_Castile" title="Alfonso VIII of Castile">Alfonso VIII of Castile</a> and a niece of King <a href="/wiki/Valdemar_II_of_Denmark" title="Valdemar II of Denmark">Valdemar II of Denmark</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor1909_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor1909-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> At that time the south of France was the stronghold of the <a href="/wiki/Catharism" title="Catharism">Cathar</a> movement. The Cathars (also known as Albigensians, due to their stronghold in <a href="/wiki/Albi" title="Albi">Albi</a>, France) were considered a <a href="/wiki/Christian_heresy" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian heresy">heretical</a> neo-<a href="/wiki/Gnostic" class="mw-redirect" title="Gnostic">gnostic</a> sect. They believed that matter was evil and only the spirit was good; this was a fundamental challenge to the notion of the <a href="/wiki/Incarnation_(Christianity)" title="Incarnation (Christianity)">incarnation</a>, central to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_theology" title="Catholic theology">Catholic theology</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Albigensian_Crusade" title="Albigensian Crusade">Albigensian Crusade</a> (1209–1229) was a 20-year military campaign initiated by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_III" title="Pope Innocent III">Pope Innocent III</a> to eliminate <a href="/wiki/Catharism" title="Catharism">Catharism</a> in <a href="/wiki/Languedoc" title="Languedoc">Languedoc</a>, in southern <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>.
</p><p>Dominic saw the need for a response that would attempt to sway members of the Albigensian movement back to mainstream Catholic thought.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Dominic became inspired to achieve this by preaching and teaching, starting near <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>, since the Albigensian Christians refused to compromise their principles despite the overwhelming force of the <a href="/wiki/Albigensian_Crusade" title="Albigensian Crusade">crusades</a> brought against them. Diego suggested another reason that was possibly aiding the spread of the reform movement. The representatives of the Catholic Church acted and moved with an offensive amount of pomp and ceremony. In contrast, the <a href="/wiki/Catharism" title="Catharism">Cathars</a> generally led <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic lifestyles</a>. To try persuasion in place of persecution, Diego suggested that the regional papal legates begin to live a reformed apostolic life. The legates agreed to the proposed changes if they could find a strong leader who could meet the Albigensians on their own ground.
</p><p>The prior took up the challenge, and he and Dominic dedicated themselves to the conversion of the Cathars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197523_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197523-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Despite this particular mission, Dominic met limited success converting Cathars by persuasion, "for though in his ten years of preaching a large number of converts were made, it has to be said that the results were not such as had been hoped for".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButler1911401–402_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButler1911401–402-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The differences in religious principles of the Albigensians called for far greater reforms than moderated appearances.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dominican_convent_established">Dominican convent established</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Dominican convent established">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Dominic became the spiritual father to several Albigensian women he had reconciled to the faith, and in 1206 he established them in a convent in <a href="/wiki/Prouille" class="mw-redirect" title="Prouille">Prouille</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-op.org_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-op.org-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> This convent would become the foundation of the Dominican nuns, thus making the Dominican nuns older than the Dominican friars. Diego sanctioned the building of a monastery for girls whose parents had sent them to the care of the Albigensians because their families were too poor to fulfill their basic needs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell198254–55_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell198254–55-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The monastery in <a href="/wiki/Prouille" class="mw-redirect" title="Prouille">Prouille</a> would later become Dominic's headquarters for his missionary effort. After two years on the mission field, Diego died while traveling back to Spain.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Dominic founded the Dominican Order in 1215. Dominic established a religious community in <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a> in 1214, to be governed by the <a href="/wiki/Rule_of_Saint_Augustine" title="Rule of Saint Augustine">rule of Saint Augustine</a> and statutes to govern the life of the friars, including the Primitive Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor191748_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor191748-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The founding documents establish that the order was founded for two purposes: preaching and the salvation of souls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Henri-Dominique_Lacordaire" class="mw-redirect" title="Henri-Dominique Lacordaire">Henri-Dominique Lacordaire</a> noted that the statutes had similarities with the constitutions of the <a href="/wiki/Premonstratensians" title="Premonstratensians">Premonstratensians</a>, indicating that Dominic had drawn inspiration from the reform of Prémontré.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Middle_Ages">Middle Ages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Middle Ages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_(maison_Seilhan)_-_panoramio.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg/220px-La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg/330px-La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg/440px-La_chambre_de_Saint_Dominique_%28maison_Seilhan%29_-_panoramio.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4752" data-file-height="3168" /></a><figcaption>Dominic's room at Maison Seilhan, in <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>, is considered the place where the Order was born.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In July 1215, with the approbation of <a href="/wiki/Folquet_de_Marselha" title="Folquet de Marselha">Bishop Foulques of Toulouse</a>, Dominic ordered his followers into an institutional life. Its purpose was revolutionary in the pastoral ministry of the Catholic Church. These priests were organized and well trained in religious studies. Dominic needed a framework—a rule—to organize these components. The Rule of Saint Augustine was an obvious choice for the Dominican Order, according to Dominic's successor Jordan of Saxony, in the <a href="/wiki/Libellus_de_principiis" title="Libellus de principiis">Libellus de principiis</a>, because it lent itself to the "salvation of souls through preaching".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197544_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197544-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> By this choice, however, the Dominican brothers designated themselves not monks, but <a href="/wiki/Canons_regular" class="mw-redirect" title="Canons regular">canons regular</a>. They could practice ministry and common life while existing in individual poverty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197544_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch197544-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell198255_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell198255-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Order of Preachers was approved in December 1216 and January 1217 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Honorius_III" title="Pope Honorius III">Pope Honorius III</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bulls</a> <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Religiosam_vitam" title="Religiosam vitam">Religiosam vitam</a></i></span> and <i><a href="/wiki/Nos_attendentes" title="Nos attendentes">Nos attendentes</a></i>. On January 21, 1217, Honorius issued the bull <i>Gratiarum omnium</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDugganGreatrexBoltonBoyle2005202_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDugganGreatrexBoltonBoyle2005202-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> recognizing Dominic's followers as an order dedicated to study and universally authorized to preach, a power formerly reserved to local episcopal authorization.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERenard1977_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERenard1977-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Along with charity, the other concept that most defines the work and spirituality of the order is study, the method most used by the Dominicans in working to defend the Church against the perils it faced. In Dominic's thinking, it was impossible for men to preach what they did not or could not understand. On August 15, 1217, Dominic dispatched seven of his followers to the great university center of Paris to establish a <a href="/wiki/Priory" title="Priory">priory</a> focused on study and preaching. The Convent of St. Jacques would eventually become the order's first <i><a href="/wiki/Studium_generale" title="Studium generale">studium generale</a></i>. Dominic was to establish similar foundations at other university towns of the day, <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> in 1218, <a href="/wiki/Palencia" title="Palencia">Palencia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Montpellier" title="Montpellier">Montpellier</a> in 1220, and <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a> just before his death in 1221.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeisheipl1960_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeisheipl1960-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The women of the order also established schools for the children of the local gentry.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg/220px-The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="320" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg/330px-The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg/440px-The_epitaph_of_the_preacher_Berthold_de_Wyrbna_in_Szprotawa_Poland.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1822" data-file-height="2654" /></a><figcaption>Dominican epitaph of Berthold de Wyrbna from 1316 on the tower of the parish church in <a href="/wiki/Szprotawa" title="Szprotawa">Szprotawa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg/220px-Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="263" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg/330px-Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg/440px-Saint_Thomas_Aquinas_Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="956" /></a><figcaption>Doctor Angelicus, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a> (1225–1274), considered by many Catholics to be the greatest Catholic theologian, is girded by angels with a mystical belt of purity after his <a href="/wiki/Chastity" title="Chastity">proof of chastity</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-Miguel_Cabrera_-_Allegory_of_the_Virgin_Patroness_of_the_Dominicans_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2156" data-file-height="2364" /></a><figcaption><i>Allegory of the Virgin Patroness of the Dominicans</i> by <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Cabrera_(painter)" title="Miguel Cabrera (painter)">Miguel Cabrera</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1219 Pope Honorius III invited Dominic and his companions to take up residence at the ancient Roman <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">basilica</a> of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Sabina" title="Santa Sabina">Santa Sabina</a>, which they did by early 1220. Before that time the friars had only a temporary residence in Rome at the convent of <a href="/wiki/San_Sisto_Vecchio" title="San Sisto Vecchio">San Sisto Vecchio</a> which Honorius III had given to Dominic circa 1218 intending it to become a convent for a reformation of nuns at Rome under Dominic's guidance. In May 1220 at Bologna the order's first <a href="/wiki/Chapter_(religion)#General_chapter" title="Chapter (religion)">General Chapter</a> mandated that each new priory of the order maintain its own <i>studium conventuale</i>, thus laying the foundation of the Dominican tradition of sponsoring widespread institutions of learning.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975Ch_1_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975Ch_1-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHastingsSelbieGray1919701_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastingsSelbieGray1919701-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The official foundation of the Dominican convent at Santa Sabina with its <i>studium conventuale</i> occurred with the legal transfer of property from Honorius III to the Order of Preachers on June 5, 1222.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1944Ch._III,_note_50_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1944Ch._III,_note_50-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> This <i>studium</i> was transformed into the order's first <i>studium provinciale</i> by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a> in 1265. Part of the curriculum of this <i>studium</i> was relocated in 1288 at the <i>studium</i> of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a> which in the 16th century world be transformed into the College of Saint Thomas (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Collegium Divi Thomæ</i>). In the 20th century the college would be relocated to the convent of <a href="/wiki/Santi_Domenico_e_Sisto" title="Santi Domenico e Sisto">Saints Dominic and Sixtus</a> and would be transformed into the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_University_of_Saint_Thomas_Aquinas" title="Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas">Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas, <i>Angelicum</i></a>.
</p><p>The Dominican friars quickly spread, including to England, where they appeared in <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a> in 1221.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan2010748_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan2010748-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In the 13th century the order reached all classes of Christian society, fought <a href="/wiki/Christian_heresy" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian heresy">heresy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Schism_(religion)" class="mw-redirect" title="Schism (religion)">schism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Paganism" title="Paganism">paganism</a> by word and book, and by its missions to the north of Europe, to Africa, and Asia passed beyond the frontiers of <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a>. Its schools spread throughout the entire Church; its doctors wrote monumental works in all branches of knowledge, including the extremely important <a href="/wiki/Albertus_Magnus" title="Albertus Magnus">Albertus Magnus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>. Its members included popes, cardinals, bishops, legates, inquisitors, confessors of princes, ambassadors, and <i>paciarii</i> (enforcers of the peace decreed by popes or councils).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The order's origins in battling heterodoxy influenced its later development and reputation. Many later Dominicans battled heresy as part of their apostolate. Indeed, many years after Dominic reacted to the Cathars, the first <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Grand Inquistor of Spain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_de_Torquemada" title="Tomás de Torquemada">Tomás de Torquemada</a>, would be drawn from the Dominican Order. The order was appointed by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_IX" title="Pope Gregory IX">Pope Gregory IX</a> the duty to carry out the <a href="/wiki/Inquisition" title="Inquisition">Inquisition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Helden1995_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Helden1995-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Torture was not regarded as a mode of punishment, but as a means of eliciting the truth. In his papal bull <i><a href="/wiki/Ad_extirpanda" title="Ad extirpanda">Ad extirpanda</a></i> of 1252, Pope Innocent IV authorised the Dominicans' use of torture under prescribed circumstances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlötzer1910_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlötzer1910-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The expansion of the order produced changes. A smaller emphasis on doctrinal activity favoured the development here and there of the <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Contemplative" class="mw-redirect" title="Contemplative">contemplative</a> life and there sprang up, especially in Germany and Italy, the mystical movement with which the names of <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a>, <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Suso" class="mw-redirect" title="Heinrich Suso">Heinrich Suso</a>, <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Tauler" title="Johannes Tauler">Johannes Tauler</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a> are associated. (See <a href="/wiki/German_mysticism" class="mw-redirect" title="German mysticism">German mysticism</a>, which has also been called "Dominican mysticism".) This movement was the prelude to the reforms undertaken, at the end of the century, by <a href="/wiki/Raimondo_delle_Vigne" class="mw-redirect" title="Raimondo delle Vigne">Raymond of Capua</a>, and continued in the following century. 
</p><p>At the same time the order found itself face to face with the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a>. It struggled against pagan tendencies in <a href="/wiki/Renaissance_humanism" title="Renaissance humanism">Renaissance humanism</a>, in Italy through Dominici and Savonarola, in Germany through the theologians of <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a> but it also furnished humanism with such advanced writers as <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Colonna_(writer)" title="Francesco Colonna (writer)">Francesco Colonna</a> (probably the writer of the <i><a href="/wiki/Hypnerotomachia_Poliphili" title="Hypnerotomachia Poliphili">Hypnerotomachia Poliphili</a></i>) and <a href="/wiki/Matteo_Bandello" title="Matteo Bandello">Matteo Bandello</a>. Many Dominicans took part in the artistic activity of the age, the most prominent being <a href="/wiki/Fra_Angelico" title="Fra Angelico">Fra Angelico</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fra_Bartolomeo" title="Fra Bartolomeo">Fra Bartolomeo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Women">Women</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Women">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Although Dominic and the early brethren had instituted female Dominican houses at Prouille and other places by 1227, houses of women attached to the Order became so popular that some of the friars had misgivings about the increasing demands of female religious establishments on their time and resources. Nonetheless, women's houses dotted the countryside throughout Europe. There were seventy-four Dominican female houses in Germany, forty-two in Italy, nine in France, eight in Spain, six in Bohemia, three in Hungary, and three in Poland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200113_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200113-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Many of the German religious houses that lodged women had been home to communities of women, such as <a href="/wiki/Beguines" class="mw-redirect" title="Beguines">Beguines</a>, that became Dominican once they were taught by the traveling preachers and put under the jurisdiction of the Dominican authoritative structure. A number of these houses became centers of study and mystical spirituality in the 14th century, as expressed in works such as the <a href="/wiki/Sister-books" title="Sister-books">sister-books</a>. There were one hundred and fifty-seven nunneries in the order by 1358. After that year, the number lessened considerably due to the Black Death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200114_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200114-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In places besides Germany, convents were founded as retreats from the world for women of the upper classes. These were original projects funded by wealthy patrons. Among these was Countess Margaret of Flanders who established the monastery of Lille, while <a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_of_Val-Duchesse#History" title="Château of Val-Duchesse">Val-Duchesse</a> at Oudergem near Brussels was built with the wealth of Adelaide of Burgundy, Duchess of Brabant (1262).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975337_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975337-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Female houses differed from male Dominican houses in that they were enclosed. The sisters chanted the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Divine Office</a> and kept all the monastic observances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200170–73_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200170–73-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The nuns lived under the authority of the general and provincial chapters of the order. They shared in all the applicable privileges of the order. The friars served as their confessors, priests, teachers and spiritual mentors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975382_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975382-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Women could be professed to the Dominican religious life at the age of thirteen. The formula for profession contained in the Constitutions of Montargis Priory (1250) requires that nuns pledge obedience to God, the Blessed Virgin, their prioress and her successors according to the Rule of Saint Augustine and the institute of the order, until death. The clothing of the sisters consisted of a white tunic and scapular, a leather belt, a black mantle, and a black veil. Candidates to profession were questioned to reveal whether they were actually married women who had merely separated from their husbands. Their intellectual abilities were also tested. Nuns were to be silent in places of prayer, the cloister, the dormitory, and refectory. Silence was maintained unless the prioress granted an exception for a specific cause. Speaking was allowed in the common parlor, but it was subordinate to strict rules, and the prioress, subprioress or other senior nun had to be present.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200130_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200130-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As well as sewing, embroidery and other genteel pursuits, the nuns participated in a number of intellectual activities, including reading and discussing pious literature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200131_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200131-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In the Strassburg monastery of Saint Margaret, some of the nuns could converse fluently in Latin. Learning still had an elevated place in the lives of these religious. In fact, Margarette Reglerin, a daughter of a wealthy Nuremberg family, was dismissed from a convent because she did not have the ability or will to learn.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975384_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975384-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="English_Province">English Province</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: English Province">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The English <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_province#Religious_institutes" title="Ecclesiastical province">Province</a> and the Hungarian Province both date back to the second general chapter of the Dominican Order, held in Bologna during the spring of 1221.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dominic dispatched twelve friars to England under the guidance of their English prior, Gilbert of Fresney, and they landed in <a href="/wiki/Dover" title="Dover">Dover</a> on August 5, 1221. The province officially came into being at its first provincial chapter in 1230.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19511_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19511-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The English Province was a component of the international order from which it obtained its laws, direction, and instructions. It was also, however, a group of Englishmen. Its direct supervisors were from England, and the members of the English Province dwelt and labored in English cities, towns, villages, and roadways. English and European ingredients constantly came in contact. The international side of the province's existence influenced the national, and the national responded to, adapted, and sometimes constrained the international.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19512_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19512-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first Dominican site in England was at Oxford, in the parishes of St. Edward and St. Adelaide.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19514_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19514-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The friars built an oratory to the Blessed Virgin Mary<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19516_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19516-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and by 1265, the brethren, in keeping with their devotion to study, began erecting a school. Actually, the Dominican brothers likely began a school immediately after their arrival, as priories were legally schools.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19518–9_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch19518–9-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Information about the schools of the English Province is limited, but a few facts are known. Much of the information available is taken from visitation records.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198032_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198032-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The "visitation" was an inspection of the province by which visitors to each priory could describe the state of its religious life and its studies at the next chapter. There were four such visits in England and Wales—Oxford, London, Cambridge and York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198033_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198033-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> All Dominican students were required to learn grammar, old and new logic, natural philosophy and theology. Of all of the curricular areas, however, theology was the most important.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198057_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll198057-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Dartford_Priory" class="mw-redirect" title="Dartford Priory">Dartford Priory</a> was established long after the primary period of monastic foundation in England had ended. It emulated, then, the monasteries found in Europe—mainly France and German—as well as the monastic traditions of their English Dominican brothers. The first nuns to inhabit Dartford were sent from the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Priory_of_Poissy&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Priory of Poissy (page does not exist)">priory of Poissy</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prieur%C3%A9_Saint-Louis_de_Poissy" class="extiw" title="fr:Prieuré Saint-Louis de Poissy">fr</a>&#93;</span> in France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee200113_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200113-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Even on the eve of the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_monasteries" title="Dissolution of the monasteries">Dissolution</a>, Prioress Jane Vane wrote to Cromwell on behalf of a postulant, saying that though she had not actually been professed, she was professed in her heart and in the eyes of God. Profession in Dartford Priory seems, then, to have been made based on personal commitment, and one's personal association with God.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As heirs of the Dominican priory of Poissy in France, the nuns of Dartford Priory in England were also heirs to a tradition of profound learning and piety. Strict discipline and plain living were characteristic of the monastery throughout its existence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPage1926181–190_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPage1926181–190-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="From_the_Reformation_to_the_French_Revolution">From the Reformation to the French Revolution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: From the Reformation to the French Revolution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg/220px-Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="298" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg/330px-Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg/440px-Fray_Bartolom%C3%A9_de_las_Casas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="1733" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_de_Las_Casas" class="mw-redirect" title="Bartolomé de Las Casas">Bartolomé de Las Casas</a> (c.1484–1566)</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_de_Las_Casas" class="mw-redirect" title="Bartolomé de Las Casas">Bartolomé de Las Casas</a>, as a settler in the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>, was galvanized by witnessing the brutal torture and genocide of the <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">Native Americans</a> by the Spanish colonists. He became famous for his advocacy of the rights of Native Americans, whose cultures, especially in the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a>, he describes with care.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagnerParish196711_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagnerParish196711-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Gaspar_da_Cruz" title="Gaspar da Cruz">Gaspar da Cruz</a> (c.1520–1570), who worked all over the Portuguese colonial empire in Asia, was probably the first Christian missionary to preach (unsuccessfully) in <a href="/wiki/Dark_ages_of_Cambodia" class="mw-redirect" title="Dark ages of Cambodia">Cambodia</a>. After a (similarly unsuccessful) stint, in 1556, in <a href="/wiki/Guangzhou#Imperial_China" title="Guangzhou">Guangzhou</a>, China, he eventually returned to Portugal and became the first European to publish a book devoted exclusively to China in 1569/1570.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELach1994742–743_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELach1994742–743-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The beginning of the 16th century confronted the order with the upheavals of Revolution. The spread of Protestantism cost it six or seven provinces and several hundreds of <a href="/wiki/Convent" title="Convent">convents</a>, but the discovery of the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a> opened up a fresh field of activity. In the 18th century, there were numerous attempts at reform, accompanied by a reduction in the number of devotees. The French Revolution ruined the order in France, and crises that more or less rapidly followed considerably lessened or wholly destroyed numerous provinces <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="From_the_19th_century_to_the_present">From the 19th century to the present</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: From the 19th century to the present">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the early 19th century, the number of Preachers seems never to have sunk below 3,500. Statistics for 1876 show 3,748, but 500 of these had been expelled from their convents and were engaged in <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/parochial" class="extiw" title="wikt:parochial">parochial</a> work. Statistics for 1910 show a total of 4,472 nominally or actually engaged in proper activities of the order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> As of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, there were 6,058 Dominican friars, including 4,470 priests.<sup id="cite_ref-ch_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ch-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
As of January&#160;2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, there are 5,753 friars overall, and 4,219 priests.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="306" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg/330px-Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg/440px-Portrait_of_Dominique_Lacordaire.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="1252" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of <a href="/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Henri_Lacordaire" title="Jean-Baptiste Henri Lacordaire">Lacordaire</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the revival movement France held a foremost place, owing to the reputation and convincing power of the orator, <a href="/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Henri_Lacordaire" title="Jean-Baptiste Henri Lacordaire">Jean-Baptiste Henri Lacordaire</a> (1802–1861). He took the habit of a Friar Preacher at Rome (1839), and the province of France was canonically erected in 1850.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScannell1910_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScannell1910-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> From this province were detached the province of <a href="/wiki/Lyon" title="Lyon">Lyon</a>, called Occitania (1862), that of <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a> (1869), and that of Canada (1909). The French restoration likewise furnished many laborers to other provinces, to assist in their organization and progress. From it came the <a href="/wiki/Master_of_the_Order_of_Preachers" title="Master of the Order of Preachers">master general</a> who remained longest at the head of the administration during the 19th century, Père <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Jandel" class="mw-redirect" title="Vincent Jandel">Vincent Jandel</a> (1850–1872). Here should be mentioned the <a href="/wiki/The_Dominican_Province_of_Saint_Joseph" class="mw-redirect" title="The Dominican Province of Saint Joseph">province of Saint Joseph in the United States</a>. Founded in 1805 by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Fenwick" title="Edward Fenwick">Edward Fenwick</a> (1768–1832), afterwards first Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Cincinnati,_Ohio" class="mw-redirect" title="Cincinnati, Ohio">Cincinnati, Ohio</a> (1821–1832). In 1905, it established the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_House_of_Studies" title="Dominican House of Studies">Dominican House of Studies</a> in <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>.
</p><p>The province of France has produced many preachers. The conferences of Notre-Dame-de-Paris were inaugurated by Père Lacordaire. The Dominicans of the province of France furnished Lacordaire (1835–1836, 1843–1851),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Monsabr%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Jacques Monsabré">Jacques Monsabré</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchroeder1911_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchroeder1911-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> and Joseph Ollivier. The pulpit of Notre Dame has been occupied by a succession of Dominicans. Père <a href="/wiki/Henri_Didon" title="Henri Didon">Henri Didon</a> (1840–1900) was a Dominican. The house of studies of the province of France publishes <i>L'Année Dominicaine</i> (founded 1859), <i>La Revue des Sciences Philosophiques et Theologiques</i> (1907), and <i>La Revue de la Jeunesse</i> (1909).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> French Dominicans founded and administer the <a href="/wiki/%C3%89cole_Biblique" title="École Biblique">École Biblique</a> et Archéologique française de Jérusalem founded in 1890 by <a href="/wiki/Marie-Joseph_Lagrange" title="Marie-Joseph Lagrange">Marie-Joseph Lagrange</a> (1855–1938), one of the leading international centres for biblical research. It is at the <i>École Biblique</i> that the famed <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem_Bible" title="Jerusalem Bible">Jerusalem Bible</a> (both editions) was prepared. Likewise Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Yves_Congar" title="Yves Congar">Yves Congar</a> was a product of the French province of the Order of Preachers.
</p><p>Doctrinal development has had an important place in the restoration of the Preachers. Several institutions, besides those already mentioned, played important parts. Such is the Biblical school at <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, open to the religious of the order and to secular clerics, which publishes the <i>Revue Biblique.</i> The <i>Pontificium Collegium Internationale Angelicum</i>, the future <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_University_of_Saint_Thomas_Aquinas" title="Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas">Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas</a> (<i>Angelicum</i>) established in Rome in 1908 by Master <a href="/wiki/Hyacinth_Cormier" class="mw-redirect" title="Hyacinth Cormier">Hyacinth Cormier</a>, opened its doors to regulars and seculars for the study of the sacred sciences. In addition to the reviews above are the <i>Revue Thomiste,</i> founded by Père Thomas Coconnier (d. 1908), and the <i>Analecta Ordinis Prædicatorum</i> (1893). Among numerous writers of the order in this period are: Cardinals <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Zigliara" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Zigliara">Thomas Zigliara</a> (d. 1893) and Zephirin González (d. 1894), two esteemed philosophers; <a href="/w/index.php?title=Alberto_Guillelmotti&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Alberto Guillelmotti (page does not exist)">Alberto Guillelmotti</a> (d. 1893), historian of the Pontifical Navy, and historian <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Denifle" class="mw-redirect" title="Heinrich Denifle">Heinrich Denifle</a> (d. 1905).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911_5-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandonnet1911-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the Reformation, many of the convents of Dominican nuns were forced to close. One which managed to survive, and afterwards founded many new houses, was St Ursula's in Augsburg. In the seventeenth century, convents of Dominican women were often asked by their bishops to undertake apostolic work, particularly educating girls and visiting the sick. St Ursula's returned to an enclosed life in the eighteenth century, but in the nineteenth century, after Napoleon had closed many European convents, King Louis I of Bavaria in 1828 restored the Religious Orders of women in his realm, provided that the nuns undertook some active work useful to the State (usually teaching or nursing).<sup id="cite_ref-kwtdominicans.co.za_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kwtdominicans.co.za-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In 1877, Bishop Ricards in South Africa requested that Augsburg send a group of nuns to start a teaching mission in King Williamstown.<sup id="cite_ref-dominicanmissionarysisters.org_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominicanmissionarysisters.org-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> From this mission were founded many Third Order Regular congregations of Dominican sisters, with their own constitutions, though still following the Rule of Saint Augustine and affiliated to the Dominican Order. These include the Dominican Sisters of Oakford, KwazuluNatal (1881),<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> the Dominican Missionary Sisters, Zimbabwe, (1890)<sup id="cite_ref-dominicanmissionarysisters.org_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominicanmissionarysisters.org-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and the Dominican Sisters of Newcastle, KwazuluNatal (1891).<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Order has influenced the formation of other Orders outside of the Roman Catholic Church, such as the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Order_of_Preachers" title="Anglican Order of Preachers">Anglican Order of Preachers</a> which is a Dominican Order within the worldwide Anglican Communion. Since not all members are obliged to take solemn or simple vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience, it operates more like a <a href="/wiki/Third_order" title="Third order">third order</a> with a third order style structure, with no contemporary or canonical ties to the historical order founded by Dominic of Guzman.<sup id="cite_ref-dominicanfriars.org_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominicanfriars.org-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Missions_abroad">Missions abroad</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Missions abroad">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Pax Mongolica of the 13th and 14th centuries that united vast parts of the European-Asian continents enabled western missionaries to travel east. "Dominican friars were preaching the Gospel on the Volga Steppes by 1225 (the year following the establishment of the Kipchak Khanate by Batu), and in 1240 Pope Gregory IX despatched others to Persia and Armenia."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarsh-Edwards1937599_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsh-Edwards1937599-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The most famous Dominican was <a href="/wiki/Jordanus" title="Jordanus">Jordanus</a> de Severac who was sent first to Persia then in 1321, together with a companion (Nicolas of Pistoia) to India. Jordanus' work and observations are recorded in two letters he wrote to the friars of Armenia, and a book, <i>Mirabilia</i>, translated as <i>Wonders of the East</i>.
</p><p>Another Dominican, <a href="/wiki/Riccoldo_da_Monte_di_Croce" title="Riccoldo da Monte di Croce">Ricold of Monte Croce</a>, worked in Syria and Persia. His travels took him from Acre to Tabriz, and on to Baghdad. There "he was welcomed by the Dominican fathers already there, and with them entered into a disputation with the Nestorians."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarsh-Edwards1937603_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsh-Edwards1937603-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Although a number of Dominicans and Franciscans persevered against the growing faith of Islam throughout the region, all Christian missionaries were soon expelled with <a href="/wiki/Timur" title="Timur">Timur</a>'s death in 1405.
</p><p>By the 1850s, the Dominicans had half a million followers in the Philippines and well-established missions in the Chinese province of <a href="/wiki/Fujian" title="Fujian">Fujian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tonkin" title="Tonkin">Tonkin</a>, Vietnam, performing thousands of baptisms each year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBowring1859211,_213_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowring1859211,_213-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>  The Dominicans presence in the Philippines has become one of the leading proponents of education with the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Colegio_de_San_Juan_de_Letran" title="Colegio de San Juan de Letran">Colegio de San Juan de Letran</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Cornell_Law_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cornell_Law-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Divisions">Divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Friars, Nuns and Third Orders form the Order of Preachers. Together with the Members of Priestly Fraternities of Saint Dominic, Dominican Laity and Dominican Youths they form the Dominican family.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Governance">Governance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Governance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The highest authority within the Order of Preachers is the <b>General Chapter</b>, which is empowered to develop legislation governing all organizations within the Dominican umbrella, as well as enforce that legislation. The General Chapter is composed of two bodies, the Chapter of Provincials and the Chapter of Definitors (or <i>Diffinitors</i>), a unique configuration within the Catholic Church. Each body is of equal authority to propose legislation and discuss other matters of general importance within the order, and each body may be called individually or jointly. The Provincials consists of the superiors of individual Dominican provinces, while the Diffinitors consists of "grass root" representatives of each province, so created to avoid provincial superiors having to spend excessive time away from their day-to-day duties of governing. To maintain stability of the legislation of the order, new legislation is enacted only when approved by three successive meetings of the General Chapter.<sup id="cite_ref-D&#39;amato_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-D&#39;amato-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first General Chapters were held at Pentecost in the years 1220 and 1221.<sup id="cite_ref-rel22_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rel22-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> More recent General Chapters have been held as follows:
</p>
<ul><li>2004 – <a href="/wiki/Krak%C3%B3w" title="Kraków">Kraków</a><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>2010 – 290th General Chapter, 1–21 September 2010, Rome,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> which elaborated the <i>mission mandates</i> of the order.<sup id="cite_ref-rel22_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rel22-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>2016 – Bologna <sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>2019 – Elective General Chapter, 9 July – 4 August 2019, at the Convent of St. Martin de Porres, <a href="/wiki/Bi%C3%AAn_H%C3%B2a" title="Biên Hòa">Biên Hòa</a>, Viet Nam</li>
<li>2022 – 16 July – 8 August 2022, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Tultenango&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Tultenango (page does not exist)">Tultenango</a> in the <a href="/wiki/State_of_Mexico" title="State of Mexico">State of Mexico</a>, a Chapter of Definitors.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>The General Chapter elects a <a href="/wiki/Master_of_the_Order_of_Preachers" title="Master of the Order of Preachers">Master of the Order</a>, who has "broad and direct authority over every brother, convent and province, and over every nun and monastery".<sup id="cite_ref-MasterOP_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MasterOP-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The master is considered the successor of Dominic, the first Master of the Order, who envisioned the office to be one of service to the community. The master is currently elected for a 9-year term, and is aided by the General Curia of the Order. His authority is subject only to the General Chapter.<sup id="cite_ref-MasterOP_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MasterOP-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> He, along with the General Chapter, may assign members, and appoint or remove superiors and other officials for the good of the order.<sup id="cite_ref-MasterOP_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MasterOP-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-D&#39;amato_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-D&#39;amato-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nuns">Nuns</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Nuns">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Dominican nuns were founded by Dominic even before he had established the friars. They are contemplatives in the cloistered life. The nuns celebrated their 800th anniversary in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Some monasteries raise funds for their operations by producing religious articles such as priestly vestments or baking communion wafers.<sup id="cite_ref-OPWest_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OPWest-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Friars">Friars</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Friars">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Friars are male members of the order, and consist of members ordained to the priesthood as well as non-ordained members, known as cooperator brothers. Both priests and cooperators participate in a variety of ministries, including preaching, parish assignments, educational ministries, social work, and related fields.<sup id="cite_ref-OPWest_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OPWest-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Dominican life is organized into four pillars that define the order's chrism: prayer, study, community and preaching.<sup id="cite_ref-rosaryHS_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rosaryHS-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Dominicans are known for their intellectual rigor that informs their preaching, as well as engaging in academic debate with contemporary scholars.<sup id="cite_ref-IntelHearaldUk_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IntelHearaldUk-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> A significant period of academic study is required prior to taking final vows of membership.<sup id="cite_ref-OPvocations_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OPvocations-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sisters"><span class="anchor" id="Dominican_Sisters"></span> Sisters</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Sisters">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg/220px-Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg/330px-Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg/440px-Relief_SS_Domenic_and_Catherine.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Marble relief of SS Dominic and Catherine</figcaption></figure>
<p>Women have been part of the Dominican Order since the beginning, but distinct active congregations of Dominican sisters in their current form are largely a product of the nineteenth century and afterward. They draw their origins both from the Dominican nuns and the communities of women tertiaries (laywomen) who lived in their own homes and gathered regularly to pray and study: the most famous of these was the <a href="/wiki/Mantellate_Sisters" class="mw-redirect" title="Mantellate Sisters">Mantellates</a> attached to Saint Dominic's church in Siena, to which Catherine of Siena belonged.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERitchieRonald200129_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERitchieRonald200129-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> In the seventeenth century, some European Dominican monasteries (e.g. St Ursula's, Augsburg) temporarily became no longer enclosed, so they could engage in teaching or nursing or other work in response to pressing local need. Any daughter houses they founded, however, became independent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGouws1978Ch._1_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGouws1978Ch._1-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> But in the nineteenth century, in response to increasing missionary fervor, monasteries were asked to send groups of women to found schools and medical clinics around the world. Large numbers of Catholic women traveled to Africa, the Americas, and the East to teach and support new communities of Catholics there, both settlers and converts. Owing to the large distances involved, these groups needed to be self-governing, and they frequently planted new self-governing congregations in neighboring mission areas in order to respond more effectively to the perceived pastoral needs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClearyMurphyMcGlynn1997_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClearyMurphyMcGlynn1997-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Following on from this period of growth in the nineteenth century, and another great period of growth in those joining these congregations in the 1950s, there are currently 24,600 Sisters belonging to 150 Dominican Religious Congregations present in 109 countries affiliated to Dominican Sisters International.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As well as the friars, Dominican sisters live their lives supported by four common values, often referred to as the Four Pillars of Dominican Life, they are community life, common prayer, study, and service. Dominic called this fourfold pattern of life "holy preaching". Henri Matisse was so moved by the care that he received from the Dominican Sisters that he collaborated in the design and interior decoration of their <a href="/wiki/Chapelle_du_Saint-Marie_du_Rosaire" class="mw-redirect" title="Chapelle du Saint-Marie du Rosaire">Chapelle du Saint-Marie du Rosaire</a> in <a href="/wiki/Vence" title="Vence">Vence</a>, France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBillot1999_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBillot1999-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Priestly_Fraternities_of_St._Dominic">Priestly Fraternities of St. Dominic</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Priestly Fraternities of St. Dominic">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Priestly Fraternities of St. Dominic <sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> consist of <a href="/wiki/Diocesan_priests" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocesan priests">diocesan priests</a> who are formally affiliated to, and "true members" of,<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> the Order of Preachers (Dominicans) through a Rule of life that they profess, and who strive for evangelical perfection under the overall direction of the Dominican friars. The origins of the Dominican fraternities can be traced from the Dominican third Order secular, which then included both priests and lay persons as members.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Now existing as a separate association from that of the laity, and with its own distinct rule to follow, the Priestly Fraternities of St. Dominic continue to be guided by the Order in embracing the gift of the spirituality of Dominic in the unique context of the diocesan priesthood. Along with the special grace of the Sacrament of Holy Orders, which helps them to perform the acts of the sacred ministry worthily, they receive new spiritual help from the profession, which makes them members of the Dominican Family and sharers in the grace and mission of the Order. While the Order provides them with these spiritual aids and directs them to their own sanctification, it leaves them free for the complete service of the local Church, under the jurisdiction of their own Bishop.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Laity">Laity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Laity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/220px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/330px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/440px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3639" data-file-height="3660" /></a><figcaption><i>The <a href="/wiki/Mystic_Marriage" class="mw-redirect" title="Mystic Marriage">Mystic Marriage</a> of Saint <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a></i> (1347–1380) by <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_di_Paolo" title="Giovanni di Paolo">Giovanni di Paolo</a>, c. 1460 (<a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art" title="Metropolitan Museum of Art">Metropolitan Museum of Art</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Lay Dominicans are governed by their own rule, the Rule of the Lay Fraternities of St. Dominic, promulgated by the Master in 1987.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> It is the fifth Rule of the Dominican Laity; the first was issued in 1285.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Lay Dominicans are also governed by the Fundamental Constitution of the Dominican Laity, and their provinces provide a General Directory and Statutes. According to their Fundamental Constitution of the Dominican Laity, sec. 4, "They have a distinctive character in both their spirituality and their service to God and neighbor. As members of the Order, they share in its apostolic mission through prayer, study and preaching according to the state of the laity."<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Pius XII, in Chosen Laymen, an Address to the Third Order of St. Dominic (1958), said, "The true condition of salvation is to meet the divine invitation by accepting the Catholic 'credo' and by observing the commandments.
