But the Lord expects more from you [Lay Dominicans], and the Church urges you to continue seeking the intimate knowledge of God and His works, to search for a more complete and valuable expression of this knowledge, a refinement of the Christian attitudes which derive from this knowledge."<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The two greatest saints among them are <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rose_of_Lima" title="Rose of Lima">Rose of Lima</a>, who lived <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a> lives in their family homes, yet both had widespread influence in their societies.
</p><p>Today, there is a growing number of Associates who share the Dominican <a href="/wiki/Charism" class="mw-redirect" title="Charism">charism</a>. Dominican Associates are Christian women and men; married, single, divorced, and widowed; clergy members and lay persons who were first drawn to and then called to live out the charism and continue the mission of the Dominican Order – to praise, to bless, to preach. Associates do not take vows, but rather make a commitment to be partners with vowed members, and to share the mission and charism of the Dominican Family in their own lives, families, churches, neighborhoods, workplaces, and cities. They are most often associated with a particular apostolic work of a congregation of active Dominican sisters.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Spirituality">Spirituality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Spirituality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Dominican emphasis on learning and charity distinguishes it from other monastic and mendicant orders. As the order first developed in Europe, learning continued to be emphasized by those friars and their sisters in Christ. These religious also struggled for a deeply-personal and intimate relationship with God. When the order reached England, many of the attributes were kept, but the English gave the order additional specialized characteristics.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Humbert_of_Romans">Humbert of Romans</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Humbert of Romans">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Humbert_of_Romans" title="Humbert of Romans">Humbert of Romans</a>, the master general of the order from 1254 to 1263, was a great administrator, preacher, and writer.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> It was under his tenure as master general that the nun of the order were given a new constitution. He also wanted his friars to reach excellence in their preaching, his most lasting contribution to the order. 
</p><p>Humbert is at the center of ascetic writers in the Dominican Order. He advised his readers,
"[Young Dominicans] are also to be instructed not to be eager to see visions or work miracles, since these avail little to salvation, and sometimes we are fooled by them; but rather they should be eager to do good in which salvation consists. Also, they should be taught not to be sad if they do not enjoy the divine consolations they hear others have; but they should know the loving Father for some reason sometimes withholds these. Again, they should learn that if they lack the grace of compunction or devotion they should not think they are not in the state of grace as long as they have good will, which is all that God regards".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWoods199837_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods199837-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshley2009240_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshley2009240-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The English Dominicans took that to heart and made it the focal point of their mysticism.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mysticism">Mysticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Mysticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Dominican Order was affected by a number of elemental influences. Its early members imbued the order with a mysticism and learning. Mysticism refers to the conviction that all believers have the capability to experience God's love. This love may manifest itself through brief ecstatic experiences, such that one may be engulfed by God and gain an immediate knowledge of him, which is unknowable through the intellect alone. Although the ultimate attainment for mysticism is union with God, the goal is just as much to become like Christ as it is to become one with Him. Those who believe in Christ should first have faith in Him without becoming engaged in such overwhelming phenomena.
</p><p>The Europeans of the order embraced ecstatic mysticism on a grand scale and looked to a union with the Creator. The English Dominicans looked for this complete unity as well but were not so focused on ecstatic experiences. Instead, their goal was to emulate the moral life of Christ more completely. The Dartford nuns were surrounded by all of those legacies and used them to create something unique. 
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Albertus_Magnus">Saint Albertus Magnus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Saint Albertus Magnus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg/170px-Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg" decoding="async" width="170" height="308" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/83/Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg 1.5x" data-file-width="227" data-file-height="411" /></a><figcaption>Painting of <a href="/wiki/Albertus_Magnus" title="Albertus Magnus">Albertus Magnus</a> (1206–1280) by <a href="/wiki/Justus_van_Gent" title="Justus van Gent">Justus van Gent</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1475</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Another member of the order who contributed significantly to its spirituality is <a href="/wiki/Albert_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Albert the Great">Albert the Great</a>, whose influence on the brotherhood permeated nearly every aspect of Dominican life.  
</p><p>Albertus Magnus championed the idea, drawn from <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_the_Areopagite" title="Dionysius the Areopagite">Dionysius the Areopagite</a>, that positive knowledge of God is possible but obscure. Thus, it is easier to state what God is not than to state what God is:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[W]e affirm things of God only relatively, that is, casually, whereas we deny things of God absolutely, that is, with reference to what He is in Himself. And there is no contradiction between a relative affirmation and an absolute negation. It is not contradictory to say that someone is white-toothed and not white.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell1982153_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell1982153-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Albert the Great wrote that wisdom and understanding enhance one's faith in God. According to him, these are the tools that God uses to commune with a contemplative. Love in the soul is both the cause and result of true understanding and judgement. It causes not only an intellectual knowledge of God, but a spiritual and emotional knowledge as well. Contemplation is the means whereby one can obtain this goal of understanding. Things that once seemed static and unchanging become full of possibility and perfection. The contemplative then knows that God is, but they do not know what God is. Thus, contemplation forever produces a mystified, imperfect knowledge of God. The soul is exalted beyond the rest of God's creation but it cannot see God himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975299_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch1975299-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell198240–95,_134–198_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell198240–95,_134–198-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Rhineland_mysticism">Rhineland mysticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Rhineland mysticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Mysticism in the <a href="/wiki/Rhineland" title="Rhineland">Rhineland</a> emerged from a series of crises—political, social (the Black Death and its consequences), and religious.<sup id="cite_ref-crsd_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crsd-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The writings of Albertus Magnus made a significant contribution to German mysticism, which became vibrant in the minds of the <a href="/wiki/Beguines_and_Beghards" title="Beguines and Beghards">Beguines</a> and women such as <a href="/wiki/Hildegard_of_Bingen" title="Hildegard of Bingen">Hildegard of Bingen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mechthild_of_Magdeburg" title="Mechthild of Magdeburg">Mechthild of Magdeburg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWoods199839_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods199839-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In Europe, it was often the female members of the order, such as <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mechthild_of_Magdeburg" title="Mechthild of Magdeburg">Mechthild of Magdeburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christine_of_Stommeln" class="mw-redirect" title="Christine of Stommeln">Christine of Stommeln</a>, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Ebner" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret Ebner">Margaret Ebner</a>, and Elsbet Stagl,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWoods1998110_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods1998110-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> who gained reputations for having mystical experiences. Notable male members of the order associated with mysticism include <a href="/wiki/Henry_Suso" title="Henry Suso">Henry Suso</a> and <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Tauler" title="Johannes Tauler">Johannes Tauler</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-crsd_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crsd-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a>'s themes is that one should be mindful of the great nobility which God has given the soul.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="English_Dominican_mysticism">English Dominican mysticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: English Dominican mysticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px .ambox-delete{border-left:10px solid #b32424}.mw-parser-output .ambox-content{border-left:10px solid #f28500}.mw-parser-output .ambox-style{border-left:10px solid #fc3}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-Unreferenced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Unreferenced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">cite</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit">improve this section</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">removed</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">March 2023</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>By 1300, the enthusiasm for preaching and conversion within the order had lessened. Mysticism, full of the ideas Albertus Magnus expostulated, became the devotion of the greatest minds and hands within the organization. It became a "powerful instrument of personal and theological transformation both within the Order of Preachers and throughout the wider reaches of Christendom.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> Although Albertus Magnus did much to instill mysticism in the Order of Preachers, it is a concept that reaches back to the Hebrew Bible. In the tradition of Holy Writ, the impossibility of coming face to face with God is a recurring motif. As time passed, Jewish and early Christian writings presented the idea of "unknowing" in which God's presence was enveloped in a dark cloud. All of those ideas associated with mysticism were at play in the spirituality of the Dominican community.
</p><p>English Dominican mysticism in the late medieval period differed from European strands of it in that, whereas European Dominican mysticism tended to concentrate on ecstatic experiences of union with the divine, English Dominican mysticism's ultimate focus was on a crucial dynamic in one's personal relationship with God. That was an essential moral imitation of the Savior as an ideal for religious change and as the means for reformation of humanity's nature as an image of divinity. This type of mysticism carried with it four elements. Firstly, spiritually it emulated the moral essence of Christ's life. Secondly, there was a connection linking moral emulation of Christ's life and humanity's disposition as images of the divine. Thirdly, English Dominican mysticism focused on an embodied spirituality with a structured love of fellow men at its center. Finally, the supreme aspiration of this mysticism was either an ethical or an actual union with God. 
</p><p>For English Dominican mystics, the mystical experience was not expressed just in one moment of the full knowledge of God but in the journey of or process of faith. That then led to an understanding that was directed toward an experiential knowledge of divinity. It is important to understand, however, that for these mystics it was possible to pursue mystical life without the visions and voices that are usually associated with such a relationship with God. 
</p><p>The centre of all mystical experience is of course Christ. English Dominicans sought to gain a full knowledge of Christ through an imitation of his life. English mystics of all types tended to focus on the moral values that the events in Christ's life exemplified. That led to a "progressive understanding of the meanings of Scripture—literal, moral, allegorical, and anagogical," which was contained within the mystical journey itself. From these considerations of Scripture comes the simplest way to imitate Christ: an emulation of the moral actions and attitudes that Jesus demonstrated in his earthly ministry becomes the most significant way to feel and have knowledge of God. 
</p><p>The English concentrated on the spirit of the events of Christ's life. They neither expected nor sought the appearance of the stigmata or any other physical manifestation. They wanted to create in themselves that environment that allowed Jesus to fulfill his divine mission, insofar as they were able. At the centre of that environment was love, which Christ showed for humanity in becoming human. Christ's love reveals the mercy of God and his care for his creation. English Dominican mystics sought through that love to become images of God. English Dominican spirituality concentrated on the moral implications of image-bearing. Love led to spiritual growth that, in turn, reflected an increase in love for God and humanity.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Devotion_to_Mary">Devotion to Mary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Devotion to Mary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Devotion to the Virgin Mary was another very important aspect of Dominican spirituality. As an order, the Dominicans believed that they were established through the good graces of Christ's mother, and through prayers, she sent missionaries to save the souls of nonbelievers. Dominican brothers and sisters unable to participate in the Divine Office sang the <a href="/wiki/Little_Office_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary" title="Little Office of the Blessed Virgin Mary">Little Office of the Blessed Virgin Mary</a> each day and saluted her as their advocate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee2001152_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2001152-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout the centuries, the Holy <a href="/wiki/Rosary" title="Rosary">Rosary</a> has been an important element among the Dominicans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBedouelle2017_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBedouelle2017-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> stated: "The Rosary of Mary is the principle and foundation on which the very Order of Saint Dominic rests for making perfect the life of its members and obtaining the salvation of others."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeeney1991_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeeney1991-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Histories of the Holy <a href="/wiki/Rosary" title="Rosary">Rosary</a> often attribute its origin to Dominic himself through the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Mary">Virgin Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeebe1996_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeebe1996-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_the_Rosary" title="Our Lady of the Rosary">Our Lady of the Rosary</a> is the title related to the <a href="/wiki/Marian_apparitions" class="mw-redirect" title="Marian apparitions">Marian apparition</a> to Dominic in 1208 in the church of <a href="/wiki/Prouille" class="mw-redirect" title="Prouille">Prouille</a> in which the Virgin Mary gave the Rosary to him. For centuries, Dominicans have been instrumental in spreading the rosary and emphasizing the Catholic belief in the <a href="/wiki/Power_of_the_rosary" class="mw-redirect" title="Power of the rosary">power of the rosary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On January 1, 2008, the master of the order declared a year of dedication to the Rosary.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_names">Other names</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Other names">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>A number of other names have been used to refer to both the order and its members.
</p>
<ul><li>In England and other countries, the Dominican friars are referred to as <b>Black Friars</b> because of the black <i>cappa</i> or cloak they wear over their white <a href="/wiki/Religious_habit" title="Religious habit">habits</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Dominicans were "Blackfriars", as opposed to "Whitefriars" (i.e., <a href="/wiki/Carmelites" title="Carmelites">Carmelites</a>) or "Greyfriars" (i.e., <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscans</a>). They are also distinct from the "Austin friars" (i.e., <a href="/wiki/Augustinians" title="Augustinians">Augustinian</a> <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Augustine" title="Order of Saint Augustine">Friars</a>) who wear a similar habit.</li>
<li>In France, the Dominicans were known as <b>Jacobins</b> because their <a href="/wiki/Couvent_des_Jacobins_de_la_rue_Saint-Jacques" title="Couvent des Jacobins de la rue Saint-Jacques">convent</a> in <a href="/wiki/Paris" title="Paris">Paris</a> was attached to the Church of Saint-Jacques, now demolished, on the way to <a href="/wiki/Saint-Jacques-du-Haut-Pas" title="Saint-Jacques-du-Haut-Pas">Saint-Jacques-du-Haut-Pas</a>, which belonged to the Italian <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_James_of_Altopascio" title="Order of Saint James of Altopascio">Order of Saint James of Altopascio</a><sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/James_the_Less" title="James the Less">James the Less</a>) <i>Sanctus Iacobus</i> in Latin.</li>
<li>Their identification as Dominicans gave rise to the pun that they were the <i><b>Domini canes</b></i>, or "Hounds of the Lord".<sup id="cite_ref-DC_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DC-2">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Mottoes">Mottoes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Mottoes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>Laudare, benedicere, praedicare</i>
<dl><dd>To praise, to bless and to preach</dd>
<dd>(from the Dominican Missal, <i>Preface of the Blessed Virgin Mary</i>)</dd></dl></li>
<li><i>Veritas</i>
<dl><dd>Truth</dd></dl></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Contemplata_aliis_tradere" title="Contemplata aliis tradere">Contemplare et contemplata aliis tradere</a></i>
<dl><dd>To study and to hand on the fruits of study (or, to contemplate and to hand on the fruits of contemplation)</dd></dl></li>
<li>One in faith, hope, and love</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notable_members">Notable members</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Notable members">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_Dominican_friars" title="List of Dominican friars">List of Dominican friars</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:40_Innocenzo_V.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/40_Innocenzo_V.jpg/220px-40_Innocenzo_V.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="381" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/40_Innocenzo_V.jpg/330px-40_Innocenzo_V.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/40_Innocenzo_V.jpg/440px-40_Innocenzo_V.jpg 2x" data-file-width="966" data-file-height="1671" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_V" title="Pope Innocent V">Pope Innocent V</a> depicted in a 1350s <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">fresco</a> by <a href="/wiki/Tommaso_da_Modena" title="Tommaso da Modena">Tommaso da Modena</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Treviso" title="Treviso">Treviso</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dominican_Popes_and_Cardinals">Dominican Popes and Cardinals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Dominican Popes and Cardinals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Four Dominican friars have served as <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">Bishop of Rome</a>:
</p>
<ul><li>Pope <a href="/wiki/Innocent_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent V">Innocent V</a> (r. 1276)</li>
<li>Pope <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XI" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XI">Benedict XI</a> (r. 1303–04)</li>
<li>Pope <a href="/wiki/Pius_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius V">Pius V</a> (r. 1566–72)</li>
<li>Pope <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIII" title="Pope Benedict XIII">Benedict XIII</a> (r. 1724–30)</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="First_elected_cardinal">First elected cardinal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: First elected cardinal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Hugh_of_Saint-Cher" title="Hugh of Saint-Cher">Hugh of Saint-Cher</a> (elected 1244) first Dominican Cardinal<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>There are three Dominican friars in the College of Cardinals:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Dominik_Duka" title="Dominik Duka">Dominik Duka</a> (b. 1943), Czech, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Prague" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Prague">Archbishop of Prague</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Christoph_Sch%C3%B6nborn" title="Christoph Schönborn">Christoph Schönborn</a> (b. 1945), Austrian, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Vienna" title="Archbishop of Vienna">Archbishop of Vienna</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jose_Advincula" title="Jose Advincula">Jose Advincula</a> (b. 1952), Filipino, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Manila" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Manila">Archbishop of Manila</a></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_Dominicans">Other Dominicans</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Other Dominicans">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Other notable Dominicans include:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Matteo_Bandello" title="Matteo Bandello">Matteo Bandello</a> (c. 1480–1562), author of novellas and soldier</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Gabriel_Barletta" title="Gabriel Barletta">Gabriel Barletta</a> (fl. 15th century), renowned preacher</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Fra_Bartolomeo" title="Fra Bartolomeo">Fra Bartolomeo</a> (1472–1517), <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance" title="Italian Renaissance">Italian Renaissance</a> painter</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Conradin_of_Bornada" title="Conradin of Bornada">Conradin of Bornada</a> (d. 1429), renowned preacher</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Vincent_of_Beauvais" title="Vincent of Beauvais">Vincent of Beauvais</a> (c. 1184-c. 1264), author/compiler of the encyclopedic text <i>The Great Mirror</i> (<i><a href="/wiki/Speculum_Maius" title="Speculum Maius">Speculum Maius</a></i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Frei_Betto" title="Frei Betto">Frei Betto</a> (b. 1945), Brazilian friar, theologian, political activist and former government adviser</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Martin_Bucer" title="Martin Bucer">Martin Bucer</a> (1491–1551), apostate who left the Order to join the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a> (c. 1260-c. 1328) German mystic and preacher</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Giordano_Bruno" title="Giordano Bruno">Giordano Bruno</a> (1548–1600), philosopher and astronomer condemned as a heretic condemned and burned in Rome by the Inquisition</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Edward_Ambrose_Burgis" title="Edward Ambrose Burgis">Edward Ambrose Burgis</a> (c. 1673–1747), historian and theologian</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Elias_Burneti_of_Bergerac" title="Elias Burneti of Bergerac">Elias Burneti of Bergerac</a> (fl. 13th century), theologian</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Anne_Buttimer" title="Anne Buttimer">Anne Buttimer</a> (1938–2017), University College Dublin</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cajetan" title="Thomas Cajetan">Thomas Cajetan</a> (1469–1534), theologian, philosopher, and cardinal, who famously debated <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tommaso_Campanella" title="Tommaso Campanella">Tommaso Campanella</a> (1568–1639), philosopher, theologian, astrologer, and poet, who was denounced by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Inquisition" title="Roman Inquisition">Inquisition</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Melchor_Cano" title="Melchor Cano">Melchor Cano</a> (1509–1560), Spanish theologian of the <a href="/wiki/School_of_Salamanca" title="School of Salamanca">School of Salamanca</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Oliviero_Carafa" title="Oliviero Carafa">Oliviero Carafa</a> (1430–1511), Italian cardinal and diplomat</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Diego_Carranza" title="Diego Carranza">Diego Carranza</a> (b. 1559), Mexican missionary</li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Marie-Dominique_Chenu" title="Marie-Dominique Chenu">Marie-Dominique Chenu</a> (1895–1990), French theologian of the <i><a href="/wiki/Nouvelle_Th%C3%A9ologie" class="mw-redirect" title="Nouvelle Théologie">Nouvelle Théologie</a></i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Richard_Luke_Concanen" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard Luke Concanen">Richard Luke Concanen</a> (1747–1810), first <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_New_York" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of New York">Bishop of New York</a></li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Anthony_Fisher" title="Anthony Fisher">Anthony Fisher</a> (b. 1960), <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Sydney" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Sydney">Archbishop of Sydney</a></li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Hermann_of_Minden" title="Hermann of Minden">Hermann of Minden</a>, 13th century <a href="/wiki/Provincial_superior" title="Provincial superior">provincial superior</a> of the German province of Dominicans</li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Michel-Louis_Gu%C3%A9rard_des_Lauriers" title="Michel-Louis Guérard des Lauriers">Michel-Louis Guérard des Lauriers</a> (1898–1988), French theologian, professor at the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Lateran_University" title="Pontifical Lateran University">Pontifical Lateran University</a> in Rome, advisor of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> on the dogma of the <a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary" title="Assumption of Mary">Assumption of Mary</a>, author of the <a href="/wiki/Sedeprivationism" title="Sedeprivationism">Thesis of Cassiciacum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sedevacantism" title="Sedevacantism">Sedevacantist</a> bishop</li></ul>

<li><a href="/wiki/St._Mary%27s_Dominican_High_School" title="St. Mary&#39;s Dominican High School">St. Mary's Dominican High School</a>, New Orleans, Louisiana, United States</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Saint_Michael_Academy_(Catarman)" title="Saint Michael Academy (Catarman)">St. Michael Academy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northern_Samar" title="Northern Samar">Northern Samar</a>, Philippines</li>
<li>St. Rose of Lima School, Bacolod City, Philippines</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Superior_Institute_of_Religious_Sciences_of_St._Thomas_Aquinas" title="Superior Institute of Religious Sciences of St. Thomas Aquinas">Superior Institute of Religious Sciences of St. Thomas Aquinas</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas" title="University of Santo Tomas">The Pontifical and Royal University of Santo Tomas</a>, The Catholic University of the Philippines – est. 1611</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Universidad_Santo_Tomas_de_Aquino" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Santo Tomas de Aquino">Universidad Santo Tomas de Aquino</a>, Bogota, Colombia</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Universidad_Santo_Tomas_de_Aquino" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Santo Tomas de Aquino">Universidad Santo Tomas de Aquino</a>, Santo Domingo, Dominican Republic, est. 1538 – First University of the New World</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas-Legazpi" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Santo Tomas-Legazpi">University of Santo Tomas-Legazpi</a> <i>(formerly Aquinas University of Legazpi)</i>, <a href="/wiki/Legazpi,_Albay" title="Legazpi, Albay">Legazpi City</a>, <a href="/wiki/Albay" title="Albay">Albay</a> – est. 1948</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Angelicum_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Angelicum College">UST-Angelicum College</a> <i>(formerly Angelicum College)</i>, <a href="/wiki/Quezon_City" title="Quezon City">Quezon City</a>, Philippines – est. 1972</li></ul>
</div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Order&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-DC-2"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-DC_2-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-DC_2-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">The reference to "<a href="/wiki/Hound" title="Hound">hounds</a>" draws on the tradition that Dominic's mother, while pregnant with him, had a vision of a black and white dog with a torch in its mouth; wherever the dog went, it set fire to the earth. It was explained that the vision was fulfilled when Dominic and his followers went forth, clad in black and white, setting fire to the earth with the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospel</a>. In English, the word "hound" has two further meanings that may be drawn upon. A hound is loyal, and the Dominicans have a reputation as obedient servants of the faith.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-friar-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-friar_4-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The word <i>friar</i> is etymologically related to the word for <i>brother</i> in <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>. <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/friar">"friar – Definition from the Merriam-Webster Online Dictionary"</a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">2008-10-21</span></span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=friar+%E2%80%93+Definition+from+the+Merriam-Webster+Online+Dictionary&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.merriam-webster.com%2Fdictionary%2Ffriar&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ADominican+Order" class="Z3988"></span> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-101"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-101">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Albertus Magnus helped shape English Dominican thought through his idea that God is knowable, but obscure. Additionally, the English friars shared his belief that wisdom and understanding enhance one's faith in God. The English Dominicans also studied classical writers. This was also part of his legacy<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWoods1998_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods1998-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
